Much of the first half of the day is spent **traveling to the venue**, especially on van or bus tours.

If time permits, band members might squeeze in personal routines – hitting a local gym, grabbing lunch, or even doing a quick parking-lot workout to stay in shape【2†L60-L67】.

Downtime in the afternoon is often used for catching up on emails, handling band business, or just relaxing before the evening’s show.

As showtime nears, the day gets busier.

By early evening (often around 6:00 or 7:00 p.m.), the venue **doors open** for the audience.

Band members might spend this time meeting fans or working the merchandise table, especially in smaller venues, to connect with the crowd【5†L78-L86】.

After the show ends, there’s a frenzy of packing up: instruments, amps, and gear are **loaded out** to trucks or vans.

Only once everything is packed does the band get to rest.

Despite the party reputation of rock tours, successful bands treat touring like a job and stick to routines.

Consistency is key to surviving the grind.

Off-days are rare, but when they occur, the band might explore a city or catch up on laundry and rest.

Everyone on board must learn bus etiquette.

Veterans share nightmare stories of someone breaking this rule and a clogged toilet **overflowing and destroying everyone’s luggage in the bus’s storage bay** – definitely not the kind of “rock ’n’ roll mayhem” anyone enjoys【6†L151-L158】.

If you’re staying up late playing video games in the front lounge, you let the driver (who sleeps in a bunk during daytime) get their rest【6†L115-L122】.

It’s common for artists to start their day handling business on their laptops or phones – responding to emails, coordinating with managers, or checking in on social media.

For instance, artist Alex White (of the band White Mystery) described waking up and immediately tackling her email inbox with a big pot of coffee, treating band business like a full-time day job【9†L59-L67】【9†L85-L93】.

Band members must be ready to do interviews, radio spot appearances, or meet-and-greets with local media, sometimes on very short notice.

It’s not unusual to squeeze in a radio interview or phone call with a journalist during the afternoon before a show.

Especially when an album release or important show is coming up, the schedule may include press events in multiple cities.

All of this happens around the main event of the concert itself. **Fan Engagement:** Beyond formal press, band members often handle fan interactions as part of their daily duties.

Many bands now offer VIP experiences or **soundcheck meet-and-greets** where a small group of fans can watch the band soundcheck and then meet them backstage【44†L132-L140】.

This means the band might spend extra time before the show talking with fans, signing merchandise, and taking photos.

These personal interactions are important for building a loyal fanbase (and yes, selling more T-shirts and albums)【44†L145-L153】.

Band members take turns being the approachable face of the group: some might thrive on chatting with anyone and everyone, while others handle behind-the-scenes tasks at that time.

Instrumentalists need to ensure their guitars, drums, keyboards, etc., are in top shape – often coordinating with their techs (if they have them) or doing maintenance themselves.

Many will change guitar strings or drumheads regularly and do quick tune-ups before showtime.

Band members often have **pre-show rituals and warm-ups**: warming up physically and mentally is essential because live shows are demanding【52†L354-L362】【52†L368-L372】.

Musicians also practice scales, run through tricky song parts, or do vocal exercises backstage right up until showtime【52†L368-L372】.

For bigger artists with a full crew, the band can focus more on artistic duties (performing, setlists, media) while crew handles logistics – but even then, the band’s **professionalism and cooperation** are a huge part of touring successfully.

Respecting that environment is every band member’s responsibility.

Newcomers quickly learn there’s a shorthand for almost everything.

Other common calls are **Soundcheck Call** (when the band needs to be at the venue for soundcheck, sometimes just called **“Band call”** on the sheet)【8†L169-L177】, and **Load-In/Load-Out** times (when the crew starts loading equipment in and when they must load out after the show)【8†L169-L177】.

After the show, load-out might be done in a rush (especially if the venue has a strict closing time). - **Soundcheck:** A rehearsal and audio check at the venue before the doors open.

Only essential personnel are present – typically the band, their techs, and the audio/lighting crew.

Often bands will request certain **backline gear** on their technical rider if flying in for a show (for example: *“Venue to provide standard 5-piece drum kit and two Marshall guitar amp stacks”*).

There are **two types of riders**: the **technical rider** and the **hospitality rider**.

The *hospitality rider* lists the band’s catering and dressing room needs – food, drinks, towels, backstage amenities.

Famous bands can have very elaborate riders, including specific brands of liquor or quirky items (the infamous **“NO Brown M&M’s”** clause – more on that later).

The rider is part of the show contract and promoters/venues are obliged to fulfill it, so it’s both a wishlist and a checklist to ensure the band is comfortable and the show can go on technically. - **Local Crew / House Crew:** These are the stagehands and technicians hired at the venue itself (often union labor in larger venues) who assist the touring crew.

They also often run spotlights or help with security, then assist with teardown at the end.

The number of local crew needed is usually advanced (planned) beforehand (e.g. *“Venue to provide 4 loaders and 2 riggers at 10am”*).

This ensures no surprises on show day.

Individual show passes (stickers or paper) might be given to local staff or guests.

Phrases like *“Do you have your laminate?”* or *“He doesn’t have a pass, don’t let him back”* are common around security checkpoints.

There’s colorful slang as well: for example, crew might refer to a nonsensical task as “juggling snowflakes” or call a friend hired as a tech as a **“bro-tech”** (hired more for who they know than what they know)【14†L75-L83】.

There’s a team of managers and crew handling different aspects of the show.

The **Artist Manager** (or band manager) oversees the band’s entire career and business.

Essentially, *“a Manager handles all things business-related regarding the artist’s career”*【21†L56-L63】.

They negotiate the guarantees and ticket revenue deals for each show and then pass those offers to the band’s manager for approval【21†L56-L63】.

They are the liaison between the band and everyone else (venue, promoter, hotel, etc.).

Next up is the **Production Manager (PM)**, who is in charge of the technical show itself.

The PM *“is responsible for all things related to getting the show up and running”* and is the *boss of the crew*, coordinating **labor calls, trucking, and timing** of the show day【23†L95-L103】.

The FOH engineer runs the mixing console in the crowd area and makes sure the band sounds great in the venue – adjusting levels, EQ, effects for the entire show【23†L105-L113】.

Each band member typically has a unique monitor mix, so the monitor engineer is juggling multiple mixes at once and adjusting on the fly if a musician signals that they need something louder or quieter.

Common tech positions include **Guitar Tech**, **Drum Tech**, **Bass Tech**, **Keyboard Tech**, etc.

A guitar tech’s day involves *“loading in, setting up, and maintaining all things related to the guitars, amps, and pedalboards”*, changing guitar strings, tuning instruments, and setting out the multiple guitars needed for different songs【23†L117-L125】.

During the show, techs stand by just offstage to hand guitars to the musicians (for example, if a guitarist breaks a string, the tech hands them a fresh guitar seamlessly).

They also **fix any gear problems on the spot** – if an amp blows out or a drum pedal breaks mid-show, the tech is the one scrambling to replace or repair it.

Backline techs are usually the first in and last out of a venue aside from the production manager – they make sure every amp, drum, and cable is working before showtime and pack them up safely after.

The techs also often handle **endorsements** (making sure the band’s gear sponsors, like guitar or drum manufacturers, are happy and the band has the equipment they need)【23†L121-L127】. - **Lighting Director (LD):** The person in charge of the concert’s lighting look.

They often call cues to spotlight operators (e.g. “spot on the singer now”), and may also be controlling video or special effects if it’s a smaller production.

On bigger tours, there might be a separate **Lighting Operator** or crew of **Lighting Techs** who set up the trusses and lights under the LD’s direction.

If there’s a **Video Director**, that role would handle any live video screens or recorded content being displayed during the show【23†L111-L118】.

They count in and count out the stock every night and coordinate restocks with the home office or merch company.

This person ensures the promoter’s interests are looked after at each show.

They handle ticket counts, verify expenses, and generally make sure the show runs according to the deal.

The list goes on – **truck drivers**, **bus drivers**, **assistants**… essentially a traveling city.

Each person has a defined role to make the whole machine work.

That’s why touring people have a reputation for being extremely organized and hard-working (and perhaps a little crazy) – it takes a special breed to live on little sleep, haul heavy gear, and solve problems in a new city each day, all for the sake of a two-hour show.

It all starts with **load-in**.

On show day, the crew arrives at the venue early (often late morning or noon) for load-in, hours before the band plays.

The headlining band usually gets the most time since they have more gear and a more complex production to set up.

At an indoor venue, the crew might have to navigate narrow loading docks or freight elevators.

Every venue is different, so advancing (pre-planning) is crucial so the crew knows if they’ll face, say, a flight of stairs or a long push from the loading bay【8†L169-L177】.

A well-advanced show will even note things like “no loading dock; street parking for truck” to avoid surprises.

Drum techs will assemble the drum kit, mic it, and line-check each drum.

Guitar techs plug in pedalboards, tune guitars, and get the amplifiers warmed up.

All this setup leads to **soundcheck time**, which is usually scheduled once the gear is ready.

The band (or at least some members) come to the venue specifically for soundcheck, often in the late afternoon.

For instance, the **day sheet** might list *“Band Soundcheck: 5:00 pm”* for the headliner, after the openers have done theirs.

They might play a verse and chorus of a familiar tune, stop, ask for adjustments, then play another song segment.

The goal is to ensure *“everything is working properly and sounding optimal”* for the show【44†L106-L114】.

Modern tours use **in-ear monitors (IEMs)** – earbud systems that allow for personalized mixes.

The monitor engineer will dial in an in-ear mix for each member (for example, drummer wants more bass guitar and click track, vocalist wants mostly their own vocals with some guitar, etc.)【44†L119-L127】.

For the band, soundcheck also serves as a warm-up and a chance to lock in any changes to the set.

They might try out a new song or make sure a particular transition works.

It’s a low-pressure run-through that catches technical issues.

For example, if the guitarist’s wireless pack is cutting out, it’s discovered at soundcheck, not mid-show.

Behind the scenes, while the band rests or does meet-and-greets, the crew double-checks everything.

They’ll often do a **quick line-check again right before the show** (especially if there were other bands’ sets after soundcheck).

Once it’s showtime, the crew’s work earlier pays off as (ideally) the show goes off without technical hitches.

Monitors and mics are disconnected, cymbals and drums are quickly deconstructed (you might notice techs starting to pack drums *during* the encore).

It’s often **past midnight** by this point – crew have been working since morning – but it has to be done efficiently to stay on schedule.

Then the **bus call** time (from the day sheet) dictates when everyone must be back on the bus to depart.

Any delay in load-out (say a stubborn piece of gear or a loading dock traffic jam) can push back departure and mess up tomorrow’s schedule, so everyone hustles.

The contract spells out how the band gets paid.

The two most common deal structures are a **guarantee** or a **door split (percentage deal)**.

A **guarantee** is a fixed fee – the band is promised a set amount of money regardless of ticket sales.

For example, a famous band might have a \$100,000 guarantee for a concert, meaning even if only half the tickets sell, they still get paid \$100K.

In a door-split, if turnout is low the band might get very little, but if it’s a packed show, the band’s pay scales up with the attendance【28†L38-L46】.

Many deals for mid-level tours are actually hybrids, commonly called “guarantee versus percentage”: e.g. *“\$20,000 vs 85% of gross after expenses”*.

This means the band is guaranteed \$20K minimum, but if the show’s ticket sales (minus agreed expenses) yield more than that, the band gets 85% of the excess.

This way, the band benefits from big sales, and the promoter still takes a cut (15% in this example).

Another variant is a **promoter profit deal**, where the promoter is allowed to keep a fixed profit (say 15% of expenses) and the rest of profit goes to the artist【26†L238-L247】.

They’ll sit down in a back office with the ticket counts, expenses, and the contract to figure out the final payout.

The promoter presents an accounting of the show – often on a **settlement sheet** that lists total tickets sold, gross income, taxes, facility fees, allowable expenses (like advertising, rental, security costs), and the net amount from which the band’s share is calculated【26†L259-L267】.

If the deal was a straight guarantee, it’s simple: the promoter hands over the guaranteed fee (minus any deposit already paid) – usually as a check or wire transfer, though sometimes in cash for smaller shows.

If it’s a percentage deal, they calculate how much is owed based on the actual sales.

For example, if it was an 85% deal and the show did \$50,000 net, the band would get \$42,500 (85%) – the promoter keeps the rest.

If there was a guarantee vs percentage, they compare the percentage amount to the guarantee and pay whichever is higher.

In the end, though, both sides sign off on the settlement sheet, acknowledging it as the final financial record.

Promoters make their profit from these deals (often a cut after expenses, or whatever margin is left if it’s a flat guarantee).

A *“percentage of the show’s expenses”* is sometimes baked in as promoter profit【26†L238-L247】.

That means the promoter essentially pays themselves a fee for putting on the show, before splitting revenue with the artist.

For big artists, their contracts can be very artist-favorable, virtually ensuring a good payday for the band.

Lesser-known acts might take more of a gamble on door deals.

In all cases, once settlement is done, that show’s earnings are final.

The venue coordinates staffing – ticketing personnel, ushers, bartenders, stagehands, loaders, security team, cleanup crew – all of whom need schedules and often are factored into the show expenses. **Security** is a big concern for any concert.

For example, at a general admission rock show, they’ll set up **barricades** and have security staff lining the front to pull out any distressed fans from the crowd surge or stop fights.

The band’s team might request additional measures if they expect a rowdy crowd (e.g. extra guards or an emergency plan).

Historical incidents have made promoters and venues very conscious of safety – for instance, after events like the **The Who’s 1979 Cincinnati tragedy** (where 11 fans were trampled in a rush for general admission seating) and the **Roskilde Festival 2000** incident (where 9 people suffocated in a crowd crush during Pearl Jam’s set), crowd management is taken extremely seriously.

The Altamont Speedway Free Concert in 1969 is a notorious example of security gone wrong – the Rolling Stones infamously hired Hells Angels bikers as security, which resulted in violence and the death of a fan【39†L69-L77】【40†L1-L4】.

Still, every concert has an element of unpredictability with large crowds, so a lot of *behind-the-scenes effort goes into keeping both the artists and audience safe*. **The Backstage Rider and Hospitality:** One fun aspect of venue operations is fulfilling the band’s **rider** – those sometimes detailed lists of backstage demands.

The rider is part of the contract, so the promoter/venue must provide the items or services agreed upon.

Common hospitality rider items include a hot meal (or high-quality catering) for the band and crew, a selection of beverages (bottled water, sodas, beer, liquor), clean towels, a private dressing room with comfortable furniture, and sometimes specific snacks.

Many bands also request local phone service or internet, especially in past decades for communication.

Some famous bands have had **extravagant or bizarre rider requests**: Van Halen’s no brown M&M’s is the classic example (they included a clause in the rider that a bowl of M&M candies be provided with all brown ones removed)【34†L223-L232】.

This wasn’t just rock star vanity – as David Lee Roth later explained, it was a test to see if the promoter had read the rider thoroughly, because their show had complicated technical requirements.

If they found even one brown M&M, it signaled that other, more important details might have been missed【34†L233-L241】.

And indeed, Van Halen would reportedly *“trash the dressing room”* or call a full technical check if the candy test failed【34†L239-L247】.

Riders can also include requests like a certain brand of bottled water (some artists insist on room-temperature Fiji water, for instance), fresh ginger root and herbal teas (common for singers), **specific foods** (one band might want vegan, gluten-free catering, another might demand steaks and lobster).

This may involve renting extra gear or arranging additional production elements.

In smaller clubs, the venue might simply tell the band “this is what we have, make it work,” but for famous acts, the contract will ensure their production needs are met or the show doesn’t happen. **Venue Revenue and Payouts:** Aside from paying the band, venues and promoters have their own income streams.

Ticket sales are the primary one – after the band’s share is accounted for, the promoter’s profit comes from whatever is left.

But venues also make significant money from **concessions**: alcohol, soft drinks, food, parking fees, and sometimes taking a percentage of the band’s merch sales (called **merch cuts**).

This is another item negotiated in advance and listed either in the rider or the promoter contract.

After the show, the merch manager does a mini-settlement with the venue to pay their cut and confirm how much was sold.

Contracts often stipulate who covers what in case of cancellation (for example, if an artist cancels last minute, often they forfeit some payment or cover expenses; if a show is canceled due to weather for an outdoor venue, there are clauses for that).

The promoter typically carries insurance for the event as well.

Lastly, **the dressing rooms backstage** operate per the contract: the venue provides what was promised (from furniture to showers).

A **hospitality runner** might be employed – someone who goes on day-of-show grocery runs to pick up that rider shopping list (cases of beer, deli trays, rare cheeses, etc.).

When everything goes to plan, the audience gets a seamless experience, unaware of the negotiations and paperwork underpinning the event.

When things go awry – perhaps a promoter sells fewer tickets than expected and loses money, or a venue fails to provide a crucial piece of gear from the rider – it can lead to behind-the-scenes drama.

The **Altamont Free Concert** is one of rock history’s darkest moments.

This was one of the deadliest concert disasters in U.S. history【46†L263-L271】.

The incident shocked the industry and led to huge changes in fire safety and pyrotechnics usage for tours (for example, strict permitting and fire marshal approval is now mandatory for any indoor pyro).

The Great White fire is a grim reminder that live entertainment can turn deadly if safety precautions are ignored – in this case, a combination of unsafe pyrotechnics and an overcrowded venue with poor fire exits proved catastrophic.
